5.3.1
Rest mass

As it was mentioned in the last section, the mass of ULDM candidates is contained in

\displaystyle 10^{-22}\>\mathrm{eV}\leq m_{\mathrm{DM}}c^{2}\lessapprox 1\>\mathrm{eV}\,.

(5.5)

5.3.2
Local energy density

Several methods exist to estimate the dark matter energy density, and this, at different scales (e.g. galactic or local)
[
1888
]
. Here, we are only interested in the DM energy density in the vicinity of the Sun, as it is what experiments on Earth will be sensitive too. Such density can be estimated by a parametric fit of the entire rotation curves of the galaxy (global method)
\rho_{\mathrm{DM}}(r)
as function of the distance
r
to the galactic center. Recent determinations of
\rho_{\mathrm{DM}}(r=r_{\odot})\equiv\rho_{\mathrm{DM}}
points towards
[
1888
]

\displaystyle\rho_{\mathrm{DM}}

\displaystyle=0.4\>\mathrm{GeV/cm}^{3}\,.

(5.6)

Eq. (
5.6
) means that the total DM energy contained in a sphere of radius
\sim 30
astronomical units (AU) (which corresponds approximately to the Sun-Neptune distance) is
\sim 10^{-13}M_{\odot}
. This shows how DM is locally subdominant, and therefore, how its gravitational impact on the orbit of the surrounding bodies is completely negligible
[
1888
]
.

5.3.3
Velocity distribution and coherence time

Galactic DM models assume that DM follows a spherical distribution around galaxies, making the so-called DM halo, as explained in Section
5.1
. During its formation, the DM halo virialized, and therefore, acquired a non-zero velocity dispersion
\sigma_{v}
. More precisely, in the heliocentric reference frame, we assume the galactic DM follows a Maxwellian velocity distribution
\mathfrak{F}(\vec{v})

[
2039
]

\displaystyle\mathfrak{F}(\vec{v})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\left(2\pi\sigma^{2}_{v}\right)^{3/2}}e^{-\frac{\left(\vec{v}-\vec{v}_{\odot}\right)^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}_{v}}}

(5.7a)

where
\vec{v}_{\odot}\equiv\vec{v}_{\mathrm{DM}}
is the mean velocity of the Solar system in the galactic frame and
\sigma_{v}
is the dispersion (virial) velocity.
Eq. (
5.7a
) can be integrated over a full sphere to become a distribution over the magnitude of the velocity, i.e
[
2150
]

\displaystyle\mathfrak{F}(v)

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi}}\frac{v}{\sigma_{v}v_{\mathrm{DM}}}e^{-\frac{v^{2}+v^{2}_{\mathrm{DM}}}{2\sigma^{2}_{v}}}\sinh\left(\frac{vv_{\mathrm{DM}}}{\sigma^{2}_{v}}\right)

(5.7b)

Since the DM frame moves compared to any laboratory frame on Earth with a velocity following the distribution Eq. (
5.7b
), the DM field itself acquires a kinetic energy
E_{k}=m_{\mathrm{DM}}v^{2}/2
in addition to its rest energy
E=m_{\mathrm{DM}}c^{2}
such that the measured DM frequency
f^{\mathrm{meas.}}_{\mathrm{DM}}
is in reality

\displaystyle f^{\mathrm{meas.}}_{\mathrm{DM}}(v)

\displaystyle=f_{\mathrm{DM}}\left(1+\frac{v^{2}}{2c^{2}}\right)\,,

(5.8a)

where

\displaystyle f_{\mathrm{DM}}

\displaystyle=\frac{m_{\mathrm{DM}}c^{2}}{2\pi\hbar}\,,

(5.8b)

is the intrinsic DM Compton frequency. In the following of this manuscript, when deriving sensitivities of experiments to ULDM fields at a given frequency
f_{\mathrm{DM}}
, the kinetic energy correction in Eq. (
5.8a
) will be neglected at leading order, such that we will consider the observed DM frequency
f^{\mathrm{meas}}_{\mathrm{DM}}
to be equal to the intrinsic DM frequency
f_{\mathrm{DM}}
.

Nonetheless, using Eqs. (
5.7b
) and (
5.8a
), we can now construct a frequency distribution which reads
[
2144
]

\displaystyle\mathfrak{F}(f^{\mathrm{meas.}}_{\mathrm{DM}})

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi}}\frac{c^{2}}{f_{\mathrm{DM}}v_{\mathrm{DM}}\sigma_{v}}e^{\frac{-v^{2}_{\mathrm{DM}}+2c^{2}\left(1-\frac{f^{\mathrm{meas.}}_{\mathrm{DM}}}{f_{\mathrm{DM}}}\right)}{2\sigma^{2}_{v}}}\sinh\left(\frac{v_{\mathrm{DM}}c}{\sigma^{2}_{v}}\sqrt{2\left(\frac{f^{\mathrm{meas.}}_{\mathrm{DM}}}{f_{\mathrm{DM}}}-1\right)}\right)\,.

(5.9)

This equation essentially means that, a more rigorous modeling of galactic DM implies that we cannot model the field as monochromatic : it is a stochastic sum of
N
different fields oscillating at different frequencies, which all follow the distribution Eq. (
5.9
)
[
2067
]
.

The frequency broadening Eq. (
5.9
) induces a characteristic coherence time of the field
\tau(\omega_{\mathrm{DM}})
. For periods shorter than the coherence time, the field behaves as monochromatic (i.e with only one amplitude and phase, but which are still stochastic), while for times longer than the coherence time, the field is a superposition of plane waves with different frequencies. One can compute the first (mean
\mu_{f^{\mathrm{meas.}}_{\mathrm{DM}}}
) and second (standard deviation
\delta f^{\mathrm{meas.}}_{\mathrm{DM}}
) moments of the frequency distribution by integration over its domain of definition (
\left[f_{\mathrm{DM}},+\infty\right[
). They are given by

\displaystyle\mu_{f^{\mathrm{meas.}}_{\mathrm{DM}}}

\displaystyle=f_{\mathrm{DM}}\left(1+\frac{v^{2}_{\mathrm{DM}}+3\sigma^{2}_{v}}{2c^{2}}\right)\approx f_{\mathrm{DM}}\,

(5.10a)

\displaystyle\delta f^{\mathrm{meas.}}_{\mathrm{DM}}

\displaystyle=\frac{f_{\mathrm{DM}}}{2c^{2}}\sqrt{v^{4}_{\mathrm{DM}}+10v^{2}_{\mathrm{DM}}\sigma^{2}_{v}+15\sigma^{4}_{v}}

(5.10b)

such that we can define the coherence time of the field as

\displaystyle\tau(f_{\mathrm{DM}})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\delta f^{\mathrm{meas.}}_{\mathrm{DM}}}=\frac{2c^{2}}{f_{\mathrm{DM}}\sqrt{v^{4}_{\mathrm{DM}}+10v^{2}_{\mathrm{DM}}\sigma^{2}_{v}+15\sigma^{4}_{v}}}\approx\frac{10^{6}}{f_{\mathrm{DM}}}\,,

(5.11)

where we used
v_{\mathrm{DM}}\sim 2.33\times 10^{5}\>\mathrm{m/s}\approx 10^{-3}\>c

[
1887
]
and
\sigma_{v}\sim v_{\mathrm{DM}}/\sqrt{2}\approx 5\times 10^{-4}\>c

[
1887
]
.
Note that for these calculations, we neglected the truncation of the velocity distribution Eq. (
5.7a
) at the galactic escape velocity
v_{\mathrm{esc}}\sim 5.5\times 10^{5}
m/s as it was done in
[
1887
]
. However, it can be shown analytically that it would lead to a slight change in mean and standard deviation of the frequency distribution (of less than 10%), i.e it will not alter significantly our estimates.

While, in the following of this manuscript, we will model DM as a single monochromatic field, and not as a superposition of fields oscillating at different frequencies following Eq. (
5.9
), we will still take into account the coherence time of the field when deriving the various sensitivities of experiments to DM, and we show here why.
Let us assume a given (linear) coupling
\zeta
between DM and any SM sector (electromagnetic, fermionic, etc..) which induces a given signal
s(t)
in our apparatus. Very generically, the signal searched for
s(t)
is

\displaystyle s(t)=\left[X_{s}\right]\zeta\cos\left(2\pi f_{\mathrm{DM}}t+\Phi\right)\,,

(5.12)

where we factorize the coupling
\zeta
from the rest of the amplitude of the signal
[X_{s}]
.
If the total time of integration of the experiment
T_{\mathrm{obs}}
is much shorter than the coherence time, i.e
T_{\mathrm{obs}}\ll\tau(f_{\mathrm{DM}})
, Eq. (
5.12
) is fully valid; i.e the signal is monochromatic, and the experiment sensitivity on the coupling at frequency
f_{\mathrm{DM}}
is simply

\displaystyle\zeta(f_{\mathrm{DM}})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{[X_{s}]}\sqrt{\frac{S_{s}(f_{\mathrm{DM}})}{T_{\mathrm{obs}}}}\equiv\frac{\sqrt{\mathrm{SNR}}}{[X_{s}]}\sqrt{\frac{S_{n}(f_{\mathrm{DM}})}{T_{\mathrm{obs}}}}\,,

(5.13a)

where
S_{s},S_{n}
are respectively the signal and noise power spectral densities (PSD) of the experiment and the SNR (signal-to-noise ratio) is defined as the ratio of signal to noise PSD, i.e
S_{s}/S_{n}
.
The SNR is used an an estimator of the signal strength, and it is useful in data analysis because it fixes a detection threshold in frequency domain, for possible discovery of new physics, compared to statistical anomaly.

Note that in this regime, where
T_{\mathrm{obs}}\ll\tau(f_{\mathrm{DM}})
, a correction factor to the sensitivity arises due to the stochastic nature of the amplitude of the field
[
1893
]
. In our case where we will always consider a 68% detection threshold (i.e. SNR = 1), this correction factor induces a loss in signal of
\sim 1.5
1
1

1

As it is pointed out in
[
1893
]
, this correction factor depends on the nature of the signal, i.e if the apparatus couples to the field itself or e.g. to its gradient. However, we will neglect this subtlety, because for gradient coupling, the exact value of this correction factor highly depends on how the sensitive axis of the experiment evolves with time, and therefore it must be calculated case by case
[
1893
]
.
.

On the other hand, when the integration time is much longer than the coherence time of the field, i.e
T_{\mathrm{obs}}\gg\tau(f_{\mathrm{DM}})
, this means that the signal searched for and parameterized by Eq. (
5.12
) is no longer coherent, i.e. it should be modeled as a sum of several stochastic harmonics, as we discussed it before. Another method to analyse the data is to cut the dataset in fragments with duration smaller than
\tau(f_{\mathrm{DM}})
and search for a coherent signal in each of these blocks of data. In such a case, the experimental sensitivity to the coupling is reduced and becomes
[
1894
]

\displaystyle\zeta(f_{\mathrm{DM}})

\displaystyle=\frac{\sqrt{\mathrm{SNR}}}{[X_{s}]}\sqrt{\frac{S_{n}(f_{\mathrm{DM}})}{\sqrt{T_{\mathrm{obs}}\tau(f_{\mathrm{DM}})}}}\,.

(5.13b)

Chapter 6
Alternatives to dark matter

As we saw in Section
5.1
, the various indicators of DM arise by using GR as our theory of gravitation and Newtonian mechanics as its small velocity and weak field limit. Another possibility to overcome the DM problem would be somehow to modify those theories of gravitation, to match again theory with observations. The most famous alternative theory to Newton’s second law is MOND (MOdified Newtonian Dynamics) introduced by Milgrom in 1983
[
1895
]
. The main idea of MOND is to explain the galaxy rotation curves by introducing an universal acceleration
a_{0}\sim 10^{-10}
m/
s^{2}

[
1895
]
in order to avoid smaller acceleration for stars very far away from center of galaxies. For a body of mass m, Newton’s second law then becomes

\displaystyle F=m\mu\left(\frac{g}{a_{0}}\right)g\,,

(6.1a)

where
g
is the gravitational field and
\mu(x)
is an asymptotic function that tends to 1 for
x\gg 1
and to
x
when
x\ll 1
. Therefore, in extreme weak acceleration environment such that
g\ll a_{0}
, Eq. (
6.1a
) becomes

\displaystyle F

\displaystyle=m\frac{g^{2}}{a_{0}}\,.

(6.1b)

A relativistic version of the MOND paradigm called TeVeS for Tensor-Vector-Scalar gravity was developed in 2004 by Bekenstein
[
1896
]
.

While the MOND theory and its extensions suffered for a long time to explain various phenomena, in particular in cluster of galaxies
[
1897
]
, and therefore would still require DM, recent studies (see e.g.
[
1898
,
1899
,
1900
]
) were able to solve these problems.
Nonetheless, in this thesis, we will be interested in solutions for dark matter involving new particles, and not through modified gravity.

Part II
Phenomenology of some ultralight dark matter candidates

Chapter 7
Cosmological evolution

7.1
Action

In this section, we will show how a light bosonic field can act as CDM at cosmological scales. In the following of this manuscript, we will be interested in several types of light fields : a scalar field
\phi
, a pseudo-scalar field
a
and a vector field
\phi^{\mu}
.

The dynamics of such fields are encoded in their action which we define as

\displaystyle S_{\phi}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{c}\int d^{4}x\sqrt{-g}\left[\frac{R}{2\kappa}-\frac{1}{2\kappa}g^{\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}\phi\partial_{\nu}\phi-\frac{V(\phi)}{2\kappa}+\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{SM}}[\Psi_{i}]+\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{int}}[\Psi_{i},\phi]\right]\,

(7.1a)

\displaystyle S_{a}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{c}\int d^{4}x\sqrt{-g}\left[\frac{R}{2\kappa}-\frac{1}{2\kappa}g^{\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}a\partial_{\nu}a-\frac{V(a)}{2\kappa}+\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{SM}}[\Psi_{i}]+\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{int}}[\Psi_{i},a]\right]\,

(7.1b)

\displaystyle S_{\phi^{\mu}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{c}\int d^{4}x\sqrt{-g}\left[\frac{R}{2\kappa}-\frac{1}{4\mu_{0}}g^{\mu\nu}g^{\alpha\beta}\phi_{\mu\alpha}\phi_{\nu\beta}-\frac{V(\phi^{\mu})}{2\mu_{0}}+\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{SM}}[\Psi_{i}]+\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{int}}[\Psi_{i},\phi^{\mu}]\right]

(7.1c)

for the scalar, pseudo-scalar and vector fields, where
\kappa=8\pi G/c^{4}
is the Einstein gravitational constant,
\mu_{0}
is the vacuum permeability,
g=\mathrm{det}(g_{\mu\nu})
and
\phi_{\mu\nu}=\partial_{\mu}\phi_{\nu}-\partial_{\nu}\phi_{\mu}
is the
\phi^{\mu}
field strength tensor. We choose a convention where the scalar
\phi
and pseudo scalar
a
fields are dimensionless, while the vector field
\phi^{\mu}
has units of
\mathrm{V.s/m}
, as the usual EM potential. In each expression, the first three terms represent the gravitational action which contains GR (through the Ricci scalar
R
) in addition to the kinetic and potential energy of the new field, while the last term represents the coupling of the new field with existing SM fields. In this section, we will focus on the former while the latter will be considered further away as it is responsible for the possible observed phenomenology for direct DM detection.

In Eq. (
7.1
), we choose a convention on the scalar field normalization factor in front of the kinetic and potential terms, such that it matches the
1/4
normalization of the vector kinetic term
1
1

1

For those respective normalization factors, one can show that integrating by parts the vector field Lagrangian leads to a term
\partial_{\mu}\phi^{\nu}\partial^{\mu}\phi_{\nu}/2
, which is similar to the scalar field kinetic term.
. Other conventions exist with a different normalization factor, e.g in
[
1989
]
, which can be recovered by a simple redefinition of the field
\phi\rightarrow\sqrt{2}\phi
.

In general, the potential for the various fields is a polynomial expansion of the field. Without loss of generality, considering a scalar field
\phi
, it has the form

\displaystyle V(\phi)=\frac{m^{2}_{\phi}c^{2}}{\hbar^{2}}\phi^{2}+\mu\phi^{3}+\lambda\phi^{4}+...

(7.2)

where the first term is the mass term with
m_{\phi}
the mass of the field. The second and third terms represent respectively cubic and quartic self interaction of the field with strength
\mu,\lambda
, in units of
[L^{-2}]
. Higher order self interactions are not represented.

In all models of interest in this manuscript, self interactions will not be considered, such that the potential of the field reduces to the mass term, with mass
m_{\phi},m_{a},m_{U}
respectively for the scalar, pseudo-scalar and vector fields. Therefore, the GR action is invariant under
\{\phi,a,\phi^{\mu}\}\rightarrow\{-\phi,-a,-\phi^{\mu}\}
parity transformation, such that both scalar and pseudo-scalar fields have the exact same dynamics at the GR level (i.e neglecting their respective interactions with SM fields). In addition, each vector field spatial component
\phi^{i}
(which are the only relevant degrees of freedom
2
2

2

Solving the equations of motion for the vector field, one can show that there is no
\ddot{\phi}^{0}
term, therefore the temporal component of the field is non dynamical. This naturally yields that its three degrees of freedom (the mass and the two polarization states) can be described by its spatial components only.
) can be described by a scalar field
\phi_{U}
, which obeys the following action

\displaystyle S^{\mathrm{Grav.}}_{\phi_{U}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{c}\int d^{4}x\sqrt{-g}\left[-\frac{1}{2\mu_{0}}g^{\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}\phi_{U}\partial_{\nu}\phi_{U}-\frac{m^{2}_{U}c^{2}}{2\mu_{0}\hbar^{2}}\phi^{2}_{U}\right]

(7.3)

in the Coulomb gauge, up to a total derivative, and neglecting GR for practical purposes. Eq. (
7.3
) has a very similar form of the action of a pure scalar field
\phi
, described in Eq. (
7.1a
) (up to a constant due to the difference in units between scalar and vector fields). Therefore, we will only derive the cosmological evolution of a pure scalar field, whose result will be easily extended to pseudo-scalar and vector fields.

7.2
Klein Gordon equation in an expanding Universe

Let us first discuss scalar fields. As noted in the end of the last section, neglecting their respective interactions with SM fields, both scalar and pseudo-scalar fields have the same dynamics, such that we will only make the calculations in the case of a pure scalar field
\phi
.
From Eq. (
7.1
), the Lagrangian describing the kinetic and potential energies of the new scalar field is

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\phi}

\displaystyle=-\frac{\sqrt{-g}}{2\kappa}\left(g^{\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}\phi\partial_{\nu}\phi+\frac{m^{2}_{\phi}c^{2}}{\hbar^{2}}\phi^{2}\right)\,

(7.4)

We represent the universe as flat, homogeneous and isotropic and described using the Friedmann-Lemaitre-Robertson-Walker (FLRW) metric defined as

\displaystyle g_{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{diag}\left(-1,a^{2}(t),a^{2}(t),a^{2}(t)\right)

(7.5)

with
a
the scale factor, such that
\sqrt{-g}=a^{3}(t)
. Using the Euler-Lagrange equation, we obtain the equation of motion of the field
\phi

\displaystyle\partial_{\mu}\left(-a^{3}(t)\partial^{\mu}\phi\right)+\frac{m^{2}_{\phi}c^{2}}{\hbar^{2}}a^{3}(t)\phi

\displaystyle=0\,

(7.6a)

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\square\phi-\frac{3H(t)}{c^{2}}\dot{\phi}-\frac{m^{2}_{\phi}c^{2}}{\hbar^{2}}\phi

\displaystyle=0

(7.6b)

where
\square=\eta^{\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}\partial_{\nu}
is the d’Alembertian operator, with
\eta_{\mu\nu}=\mathrm{diag(-1,1,1,1)}
, the Minkowski metric, the dot represents derivative with respect to time and where we defined
H(t)=\dot{a}(t)/a(t)
, the Hubble constant. As it can be seen from Eq. (
7.6b
), the unit of the field does not impact the equation of motion, since it is a linear differential equation. Therefore, the vector field will follow the exact same Klein-Gordon equation, despite its different unit.

Eq. (
7.6b
) describes a damped harmonic oscillator. At early times of the universe, when
H\gg m_{\phi}c^{2}/\hbar
, the solution of this equation is a constant
\phi\rightarrow\phi_{0}
, i.e the field is frozen at
\phi_{0}
and behaves effectively as massless with a flat potential. As soon as
H\sim m_{\phi}c^{2}/\hbar
, the potential of the field gets curved by the mass, and as long as
\phi_{0}
does not exactly correspond to the local minimum of the potential, the field rolls down the potential and gets massive. When
H\ll m_{\phi}c^{2}/\hbar
, such that we can neglect the field friction, the solution of the Klein-Gordon equation is an oscillating solution of the form

\displaystyle\phi(t,\vec{x})

\displaystyle=\phi_{0}\cos(\omega_{\phi}t-\vec{k}_{\phi}\cdot\vec{x}+\Phi)

(7.7a)

where
\phi_{0},\Phi
are respectively the amplitude of oscillation and a phase and where

\displaystyle|\vec{k}_{\phi}|=\sqrt{\omega^{2}_{\phi}/c^{2}-m^{2}_{\phi}c^{2}/\hbar^{2}}

(7.7b)

is the wavevector of the field. A vector field will oscillate in the exact same manner, but with a vectorial amplitude, i.e

\displaystyle\phi^{\mu}(t,\vec{x})

\displaystyle=Y^{\mu}\cos(\omega_{U}t-\vec{k}_{U}\cdot\vec{x}+\Phi)

(7.8)

This generation of field oscillation, with non-zero amplitude, which as we shall see in the next section leads to a non-zero energy density for the field, is more commonly known as the misalignment mechanism. Indeed, if by any chance,
\phi_{0}
corresponded to the exact potential minimum, the field would not oscillate, and as a consequence would still be effectively massless.

In the following of this manuscript, we require the field to oscillate, such that the necessary condition
H\ll m_{\phi}c^{2}/\hbar
equates to

\displaystyle m_{\phi}\gg 10^{-33}\>\mathrm{eV/c}^{2}

(7.9)

with the current value of the Hubble constant
H_{0}\sim 10^{-18}
s
-1
. Note that this condition is looser than the bound of DM mass from observations Eq. (
5.3
).

7.3
Oscillating field as cold dark matter

We now focus on the energy density and pressure of such fields, and we show that they behave at cosmological scale as cold dark matter, i.e pressureless matter with equation of state
\omega=P/\rho\sim 0
.

7.3.1
Scalar field

Still neglecting interaction, the stress-energy tensor of the scalar field
T^{\mu\nu}
can be defined from the field Lagrangian Eq. (
7.4
), as

\displaystyle T^{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{\sqrt{-g}}\frac{\delta(\sqrt{-g}\mathcal{L}_{\phi})}{\delta g_{\mu\nu}}\,

(7.10a)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{-g}\kappa\delta g_{\mu\nu}}\left(\delta(\sqrt{-g})\left(-g^{\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}\phi\partial_{\nu}\phi-\frac{m^{2}_{\phi}c^{2}}{\hbar^{2}}\phi^{2}\right)+\sqrt{-g}\left(-\delta g^{\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}\phi\partial_{\nu}\phi\right)\right)

(7.10b)

where we only vary the metric parameters. Using Jacobi’s formula, we have

\displaystyle\delta\sqrt{-g}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{-g}g^{\mu\nu}\delta g_{\mu\nu}\,

(7.11a)

\displaystyle\delta g^{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=-g^{\mu\alpha}g^{\nu\beta}\delta g_{\alpha\beta}

(7.11b)

which simplifies the stress-energy tensor expression to

\displaystyle T^{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=\frac{-g^{\mu\nu}\left(\frac{1}{2}\partial_{\alpha}\phi\partial^{\alpha}\phi+\frac{m^{2}_{\phi}c^{2}}{2\hbar^{2}}\phi^{2}\right)+\partial^{\mu}\phi\partial^{\nu}\phi}{\kappa}

(7.12)

For simplicity, we assume the field behaves as a perfect fluid at cosmological scales, such that its stress-energy tensor has the form

\displaystyle T^{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=\left(\rho+\frac{P}{c^{2}}\right)u^{\mu}u^{\nu}+Pg^{\mu\nu}

(7.13)

where
P,\rho
are respectively the pressure and energy density of the fluid and
u^{\mu}
its 4-velocity. By identification with Eq. (
7.12
), we find the average pressure over several field oscillations to be

\displaystyle\langle P\rangle

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{\kappa}\left\langle\frac{1}{2}\partial_{\alpha}\phi\partial^{\alpha}\phi+\frac{m^{2}_{\phi}c^{2}}{2\hbar^{2}}\phi^{2}\right\rangle\equiv 0\,

(7.14a)

while the average energy density is defined as the
00
component of the stress-energy tensor, i.e

\displaystyle\langle\rho\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle T_{00}\rangle=\frac{1}{\kappa}\left\langle\frac{1}{2}\partial_{\alpha}\phi\partial^{\alpha}\phi+\frac{m^{2}_{\phi}c^{2}}{2\hbar^{2}}\phi^{2}+\left(\frac{1}{c}\frac{\partial\phi}{\partial t}\right)^{2}\right\rangle\equiv\frac{\omega^{2}_{\phi}\phi^{2}_{0}}{2\kappa c^{2}}

(7.14b)

where we used Eqs.(
7.7a
), (
7.7b
), and
\langle A+B\cos(2\omega t+\Phi)\rangle=A
.
The observed equation of state of the field is defined as

\displaystyle\frac{\langle P\rangle}{\langle\rho\rangle}=0\,,

(7.15)

Therefore, the scalar field behaves cosmologically as a pressureless fluid, i.e cold dark matter with energy density

\displaystyle\langle\rho\rangle=\frac{\omega^{2}_{\phi}\phi^{2}_{0}}{2\kappa c^{2}}\equiv\rho_{\mathrm{DM}}

(7.16)

where
\rho_{\mathrm{DM}}
is the local DM energy density, such that we define the amplitude of oscillation of all scalar ULDM candidates to be

\displaystyle\phi_{0}

\displaystyle=\frac{\sqrt{16\pi G\rho_{\mathrm{DM}}}}{\omega_{\phi}c}

(7.17)

In conventions where the scalar field Lagrangian Eq. (
7.4
) is multiplied by 2 compared to ours (e.g in
[
1989
]
, with the redefinition of the field
\phi\rightarrow\sqrt{2}\phi
), the associated amplitude of the scalar field is
\phi_{0}=\sqrt{8\pi G\rho_{\mathrm{DM}}}/\omega_{\phi}c
. We shall see in Chapter
8
that the observables of interest remain unchanged, as these fields redefinitions are unphysical, as expected.

7.3.2
Vector field

From Eq. (
7.1c
), we can define the vector field Lagrangian as

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\phi^{\mu}}=

\displaystyle-\sqrt{-g}\left(\frac{1}{4\mu_{0}}g^{\mu\nu}g^{\alpha\beta}\phi_{\mu\alpha}\phi_{\nu\beta}-\frac{m^{2}_{U}c^{2}}{2\mu_{0}\hbar^{2}}g^{\mu\nu}\phi_{\mu}\phi_{\nu}\right)\,,

(7.18)

The vector field equation reads

\displaystyle\partial_{\mu}\phi^{\mu\nu}-\frac{m^{2}_{U}c^{2}}{\hbar^{2}}\phi^{\nu}=0\,,

(7.19a)

where we assumed an expanding Universe, with
H\ll m_{U}c^{2}/\hbar
, such that the Hubble constant is neglected.
Taking the divergence of Eq. (
7.19a
) and using the antisymmetric property of
\phi^{\mu\nu}
, we find the continuity equation of
\phi^{\mu}
i.e

\displaystyle\partial_{\mu}\phi^{\mu}

\displaystyle=0\,.

(7.19b)

While a similar equation can be found in electromagnetism by choosing the Lorenz gauge, Eq. (
7.19b
) is genuinely a field equation, which means that we are still free to choose a gauge.
As in the scalar field case, the stress-energy tensor of the vector field is defined as

\displaystyle T^{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{\sqrt{-g}}\frac{\delta(\sqrt{-g}\mathcal{L}_{\phi^{\mu}})}{\delta g_{\mu\nu}}=\frac{-g^{\mu\nu}\left(\frac{1}{4}\phi_{\alpha\beta}\phi^{\alpha\beta}+\frac{m^{2}_{U}c^{2}}{2\hbar^{2}}\phi_{\alpha}\phi^{\alpha}\right)+\phi^{\alpha\mu}\phi_{\alpha}^{\ \nu}+\frac{m^{2}_{U}c^{2}}{\hbar^{2}}\phi^{\mu}\phi^{\nu}}{\mu_{0}}\,,

(7.20)

where we used again the antisymmetricity property of
\phi^{\mu\nu}
. We now assume an equivalent form of the plane wave solution Eq. (
7.8
),
\phi^{\mu}=Y^{\mu}\mathcal{R}[e^{ik_{\mu}x^{\mu}}]
(forgetting about the irrelevant phase), such that each field term of Eq. (
7.20
) can be written as

\displaystyle\phi_{\alpha\beta}\phi^{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle=2\left[\left(ik_{\alpha}Y_{\beta}\mathcal{R}[e^{ik_{a}x^{a}}]\right)\left(ik^{\alpha}Y^{\beta}\mathcal{R}[e^{ik_{a}x^{a}}]\right)-\left(ik_{\beta}Y_{\alpha}\mathcal{R}[e^{ik_{a}x^{a}}]\right)\left(ik^{\alpha}Y^{\beta}\mathcal{R}[e^{ik_{a}x^{a}}]\right)\right]\,

(7.21a)

\displaystyle\phi_{\alpha}\phi^{\alpha}

\displaystyle=\left(Y_{\alpha}\mathcal{R}[e^{ik_{a}x^{a}}]\right)\left(Y^{\alpha}\mathcal{R}[e^{ik_{a}x^{a}}]\right)\,

(7.21b)

\displaystyle\phi^{\alpha\mu}\phi_{\alpha}^{\ \nu}

\displaystyle=\left(ik^{\alpha}Y^{\mu}\mathcal{R}[e^{ik_{a}x^{a}}]-ik^{\mu}Y^{\alpha}\mathcal{R}[e^{ik_{a}x^{a}}]\right)\left(ik_{\alpha}Y^{\nu}\mathcal{R}[e^{ik_{a}x^{a}}]-ik^{\nu}Y_{\alpha}\mathcal{R}[e^{ik_{a}x^{a}}]\right)\,

(7.21c)

\displaystyle\phi^{\mu}\phi^{\nu}

\displaystyle=\left(Y^{\mu}\mathcal{R}[e^{ik_{a}x^{a}}]\right)\left(Y^{\nu}\mathcal{R}[e^{ik_{a}x^{a}}]\right)\,.

(7.21d)

Considering

\displaystyle\langle\mathcal{R}[a^{\mu}e^{ik_{a}x^{a}}]\mathcal{R}[b^{\nu}e^{ik_{a}x^{a}}]\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{R}[a^{\mu}b_{*}^{\nu}]

(7.22)

where the star denotes the complex conjugate, the average over several oscillations of the stress-energy tensor of the vector field can be written as

\displaystyle\langle T^{\mu\nu}\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{-g^{\mu\nu}\left(\frac{1}{4}k_{\alpha}k^{\alpha}Y_{\beta}Y^{\beta}+\frac{m^{2}_{U}c^{2}}{4\hbar^{2}}Y_{\alpha}Y^{\alpha}\right)+\frac{1}{2}k_{\alpha}k^{\alpha}Y^{\mu}Y^{\nu}+\frac{1}{2}k^{\mu}k^{\nu}Y_{\alpha}Y^{\alpha}+\frac{m^{2}_{U}c^{2}}{2\hbar^{2}}Y^{\mu}Y^{\nu}}{\mu_{0}}

(7.23)

where we used
k_{\mu}Y^{\mu}=0
from Eq. (
7.19b
). Using the same identification with perfect fluid as before, we find that the average pressure is

\displaystyle\langle P\rangle

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{\mu_{0}}\left\langle\frac{1}{4}k_{\alpha}k^{\alpha}Y_{\beta}Y^{\beta}+\frac{m^{2}_{U}c^{2}}{4\hbar^{2}}Y_{\alpha}Y^{\alpha}\right\rangle\equiv 0

(7.24a)

since
k^{\mu}k_{\mu}=-(\omega_{U}/c)^{2}+|\vec{k}_{U}|^{2}
and using Eq. (
7.7b
). The average energy density is

\displaystyle\langle\rho\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\mu_{0}}\left\langle\frac{1}{4}k_{\alpha}k^{\alpha}Y_{\beta}Y^{\beta}+\frac{m^{2}_{U}c^{2}}{4\hbar^{2}}Y_{\alpha}Y^{\alpha}+\frac{1}{2}k_{\alpha}k^{\alpha}(Y_{0})^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{\omega_{U}}{c}\right)^{2}Y_{\alpha}Y^{\alpha}+\frac{m^{2}_{U}c^{2}}{2\hbar^{2}}(Y_{0})^{2}\right\rangle\,

\displaystyle\equiv\frac{\omega^{2}_{U}}{2\mu_{0}c^{2}}Y_{\alpha}Y^{\alpha}\,.

(7.24b)

Then, the amplitude of the total vector field is simply

\displaystyle Y_{\alpha}Y^{\alpha}

\displaystyle=\frac{2\mu_{0}\rho_{\mathrm{DM}}c^{2}}{\omega^{2}_{U}}\,.

(7.25a)

Due to the small galactic velocity in the DM halo, the temporal contribution of the dot product in the previous equation can be neglected. Indeed, parametrizing
Y^{\mu}=(\phi/c,\vec{Y})
, we have

\displaystyle Y_{\alpha}Y^{\alpha}

\displaystyle=-\left(\frac{\phi}{c}\right)^{2}+|\vec{Y}|^{2}=-\left(\frac{c}{\omega_{U}}\right)^{2}\left(\vec{k}_{U}\cdot\vec{Y}\right)^{2}+|\vec{Y}|^{2}=\frac{v^{2}_{\mathrm{DM}}}{c^{2}}\left(\hat{e}_{v}\cdot\vec{Y}\right)^{2}+|\vec{Y}|^{2}\approx|\vec{Y}|^{2}\,,

(7.25b)

where we used the continuity equation
k_{\mu}Y^{\mu}=0
to set
\phi/c=(\vec{k}_{U}\cdot\vec{Y})c/\omega_{U}
and
\vec{k}_{U}=-\omega_{U}\vec{v}_{\mathrm{DM}}/c^{2}=-\omega_{U}v_{\mathrm{DM}}\hat{e}_{v}/c^{2}
. This implies

\displaystyle|\vec{Y}|

\displaystyle=\frac{\sqrt{2\mu_{0}\rho_{\mathrm{DM}}}c}{\omega_{U}}\,.

(7.26)

Eq. (
7.26
) is a good approximation for experiments where the propagation of the field is negligible, since we assume
\vec{k}_{U}=0
, such that
Y^{0}=0
(from
k_{\mu}Y^{\mu}=0
).
Note that Eqs. (
7.17
) and (
7.26
) are related by the change
\mu_{0}\rightarrow\kappa
, as expected from the respective Lagrangian densities.

7.4
Expressing the dark matter field in various reference frames

7.4.1
Galactic frame

In the galactic frame, which is assumed to be the local DM rest frame
3
3

3

Note that we can find a rest frame of DM because the field is massive, as opposed to the electromagnetic field.
, the expression of the (scalar) field Eq. (
7.7a
) reduces to

\displaystyle\phi=\phi_{0}\cos(\omega_{\phi}t+\Phi)

(7.27)

with
\hbar\omega_{\phi}=m_{\phi}c^{2}
, since
|\vec{k}_{\phi}|=0
on average in this frame. However, for experiments lasting longer than the typical coherence time of the field Eq. (
5.11
), one must take into account the velocity distribution introduced in Eq. (
5.7a
). In this case, the mean velocity of the distribution is
v_{\odot}\rightarrow v^{\mathrm{RF}}_{\mathrm{DM}}=0
, while the frequency dispersion is given by Eq. (
5.10b
) (with
v_{\mathrm{DM}}=0
)
\delta f^{\mathrm{RF}}_{\phi}\sim 5\times 10^{-7}f_{\phi}
.

The form of the field Eq. (
7.27
) will be used in our study of atom interferometry probes of ULDM in Chapter (
13
). As mentioned previously, we will neglect the frequency broadening due to the velocity distribution Eq. (
5.7a
), such that we will consider DM as a pure monochromatic field.

7.4.2
Laboratory frame

Eq. (
7.7a
) is the exact solution of the ULDM field in any other reference frame, in particular laboratory frames on Earth. In such cases, the field is propagating with velocity
|\vec{v}_{\mathrm{DM}}|\sim 10^{-3}c
(see Table
5.1
, and neglecting the Earth velocity around the Sun,
|\vec{v}_{\mathrm{Earth}}|\sim 3\times 10^{4}
m/s, which averages to
0
over the year.).

We would like to know if it is still possible to neglect the propagation term
\vec{k}_{\phi}\cdot\vec{x}
for the sensitivity estimates of the various experiments under consideration in the following of this thesis. To answer this question, the first point is to know whether the experiment couples to the field itself or to its gradient. In the latter case, it is clear that the full form of the field Eq. (
7.7a
) must be used, otherwise its gradient becomes
0
.

In the former case, in order to neglect the propagation, and therefore considering the field as a pure standing wave, the main requirement is that the de Broglie wavelength of the field
\lambda^{\mathrm{dB}}_{\mathrm{DM}}
must be much larger than the length scale of the experiment
\ell_{\mathrm{exp}}
, i.e, in terms of DM mass

\displaystyle\ell_{\mathrm{exp}}\ll 10^{-3}\left(\frac{1\>\mathrm{eV}}{m_{\phi}c^{2}}\right)\>\mathrm{m}\,.

(7.28)

As we shall see in the corresponding chapters, this requirement is fulfilled for the experiments detailed in Chapters
9
,
10
,
11
and
12
, therefore we will assume the field to be homogeneous over the full size of experiment with the form

\displaystyle\phi=\phi_{0}\cos(\omega_{\phi}t+\Phi)

(7.29a)

with

\displaystyle\omega_{\phi}=\sqrt{\left|\vec{k}_{\phi}\right|^{2}c^{2}+\frac{m^{2}_{\phi}c^{4}}{\hbar^{2}}}=\frac{m_{\phi}c^{2}}{\hbar}\sqrt{1+\left(\frac{v_{\mathrm{DM}}}{c}\right)^{2}}\approx\frac{m_{\phi}c^{2}}{\hbar}

(7.29b)

where we used
\left|\vec{k}_{\phi}\right|=2\pi/\lambda^{\mathrm{dB}}_{\mathrm{DM}}
and where we neglected the
(v_{\mathrm{DM}}/c)^{2}\sim 10^{-6}
correction. One can easily notice that this form is equivalent (up to
(v_{\mathrm{DM}}/c)^{2}
corrections) to the one in the galactic frame Eq. (
7.27
).

In Chapter
15
, we will be interested in the direct detection of ULDM fields by a space-based experiment,
LISA
, which couples to the gradient of the field. and therefore, we will consider the exact form of the field Eq. (
7.7a
).

Chapter 8
Couplings to Standard Model fields

8.1
Scalar field

8.1.1
Theoretical motivations

A scalar particle is the simplest model of ULDM. Light scalar fields appear in various theoretical models, such as dilaton and moduli fields in string theory
[
1988
]
, or the relaxion field to understand the electroweak hierarchy problem, i.e the large energy difference between the electroweak scale (set by the Higgs field vacuum expectation value
v\sim 246
GeV) and the gravity scale (set by the Planck mass
M_{P}c^{2}\sim 10^{19}
GeV)
[
1903
]
.
As we shall see in the following, a coupling between such scalar field with SM fields naturally leads to violation of the equivalence principle, which is expected to happen at some scale, as discussed in Section
3.4
.

8.1.2
Rest mass and transition frequency oscillations

We define the linear interaction Lagrangian of a dimensionless scalar field
\phi
appearing in Eq. (
7.1a
) as
[
1989
]

\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{int}}=\frac{\phi}{\sqrt{2}}\left(\frac{d_{e}}{4\mu_{0}}F_{\mu\nu}F^{\mu\nu}-\frac{d_{g}\beta_{3}}{2g_{3}}G^{a}_{\mu\nu}G^{a\mu\nu}-\sum_{i=e,u,d}(d_{m_{i}}+\gamma_{m_{i}}d_{g})m_{i}\bar{\psi_{i}}\psi_{i}\right)\,,

(8.1)

where as stated previously, our convention in the scalar field normalization implies an additional
1/\sqrt{2}
factor compared to
[
1904
,
1989
]
.
F^{\mu\nu},G^{a\mu\nu}
represent the electromagnetic and gluonic strength tensors respectively,
\mu_{0}
is the vacuum magnetic permeability,
g_{3},\beta_{3}
are the dimensionless QCD coupling constant and QCD beta function for the running of
g_{3}
,
m_{i}
the mass of the fermions fields in units of energy, with spinors
\psi_{i}
and
\gamma_{m_{i}}
the anomalous dimension giving the energy running of the masses of the QCD coupled fermions. The
d_{i}
represent the dimensionless coupling constants between the scalar dilaton and the different matter fields.

Since the energy scale we are interested in is very low (compared to second generation leptons’ masses), this low energy effective Lagrangian involves only the low mass fermions, i.e the electron, up and down quarks, as can be noticed in the last term in Eq. (
8.1
).

These interactions between the SM particles and the dilaton lead to variation of several fundamental constants of Nature in SM, namely the QCD energy scale
\Lambda_{\mathrm{QCD}}
, the EM fine structure constant
\alpha
, the electron mass
m_{e}
and the light quark masses
m_{i}
, with
i=\{u,d\}
. Specifically, assuming the usual EM kinetic term in addition to the coupling of the dilaton to the EM sector (which is the first term of Eq. (
8.1
)), one has

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}

\displaystyle\supset-\frac{1}{4\mu_{0}}\left(1-\frac{d_{e}\phi}{\sqrt{2}}\right)F_{\mu\nu}F^{\mu\nu}\approx-\frac{1}{4\mu_{0}(1+\frac{d_{e}\phi}{\sqrt{2}})}F_{\mu\nu}F^{\mu\nu}\,,

(8.2)

where we used the Taylor expansion of the term in brackets as
d_{e}\phi\ll 1
. Since
\mu_{0}\propto\alpha
, this Lagrangian leads to a small dependency of the fine structure constant on the scalar field as

\displaystyle\alpha(\phi)

\displaystyle=\left(1+\frac{d_{e}\phi}{\sqrt{2}}\right)\alpha\,,

(8.3a)

where
\alpha
is the bare value of the fine structure constant, i.e without the dilaton-EM coupling. The other constants of Nature evolve as
[
1904
]

\displaystyle\Lambda_{3}(\phi)

\displaystyle=\left(1+\frac{d_{g}\phi}{\sqrt{2}}\right)\Lambda_{3}\,,

(8.3b)

\displaystyle m_{e}(\phi)

\displaystyle=\left(1+\frac{d_{m_{e}}\phi}{\sqrt{2}}\right)m_{e}\,,

(8.3c)

\displaystyle m_{i}(\Lambda_{\mathrm{QCD}})(\phi)

\displaystyle=\left(1+\frac{d_{m_{i}}\phi}{\sqrt{2}}\right)m_{i}(\Lambda_{\mathrm{QCD}})\,.

(8.3d)

If one of these couplings is non-zero, the corresponding variable would not be constant at two different spacetime positions where the field
\phi
takes different values, hence the violation of the local position invariance, or more generally of the equivalence principle.
Following the work of
[
1904
]
, we introduce the mean light quark mass
\hat{m}=(m_{u}+m_{d})/2
and the difference of light quark masses
\delta m=m_{d}-m_{u}
. Following Eq. (
8.3
), the numerical value of these new parameters will change as

\displaystyle\hat{m}(\phi)

\displaystyle=\left(1+\frac{d_{\hat{m}}\phi}{\sqrt{2}}\right)\hat{m}\,,

(8.3e)

\displaystyle\delta m(\phi)

\displaystyle=\left(1+\frac{d_{\delta m}\phi}{\sqrt{2}}\right)\delta m\,,

(8.3f)

with

\displaystyle d_{\hat{m}}

\displaystyle=\frac{m_{u}d_{m_{u}}+m_{d}d_{m_{d}}}{m_{u}+m_{d}},\,

(8.3g)

\displaystyle d_{\delta m}

\displaystyle=\frac{m_{d}d_{m_{d}}-m_{u}d_{m_{u}}}{m_{d}-m_{u}}.

(8.3h)

Following
[
1904
]
, we can define dimensionless dilatonic mass and frequency charges for the atom A respectively given by

\displaystyle[Q^{A}_{M}]_{d}

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial\ln m_{A}(\phi)}{\partial\phi}\left(\equiv\alpha_{A}/\sqrt{2}\right)\,,

(8.4a)

\displaystyle[Q^{A}_{\omega}]_{d}

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial\ln\omega_{A}(\phi)}{\partial\phi}\,,

(8.4b)

which encode the spacetime variation of the mass and of the transition frequency of the atom A. In Eq. (
8.4a
), we show in parenthesis the relation between our definition of the mass charge with the previously defined coupling function in
[
1904
,
1989
]
.
In a general frame (i.e where the gradient of the DM field is not neglected), using Eqs. (
7.7a
) and (
8.4
), the rest mass and transition frequency of an atom A oscillate as

\displaystyle m_{A}(t,\vec{x})

\displaystyle=m^{0}_{A}\left(1+\frac{\sqrt{16\pi G\rho_{\mathrm{DM}}}[Q^{A}_{M}]_{d}}{\omega_{\phi}c}\cos(\omega_{\phi}t-\vec{k}_{\phi}\cdot\vec{x}+\Phi)\right)\,,

(8.5a)

\displaystyle\omega_{A}(t,\vec{x})

\displaystyle=\omega^{0}_{A}\left(1+\frac{\sqrt{16\pi G\rho_{\mathrm{DM}}}[Q^{A}_{\omega}]_{d}}{\omega_{\phi}c}\cos(\omega_{\phi}t-\vec{k}_{\phi}\cdot\vec{x}+\Phi)\right)\,,

(8.5b)

where
m^{0}_{A},\omega^{0}_{A}
are respectively the unperturbed rest mass and transition frequency of A.

), (

), so it is not provided. As we shall see in Chapter

and Chapter

, we will not consider the atomic transition of

2
, reason why their frequency charges are not provided.

The rest mass depending differently on all the varying constants of Nature, we can define partial dilatonic mass charges
Q_{M,i}
such that
[
1904
]

\displaystyle[Q^{\mathrm{atom}}_{M}]_{d}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\left(Q^{\mathrm{atom}}_{M,m_{e}}(d_{m_{e}}-d_{g})+Q^{\mathrm{atom}}_{M,e}d_{e}+Q^{\mathrm{atom}}_{M,\hat{m}}(d_{\hat{m}}-d_{g})+Q^{\mathrm{atom}}_{M,\delta m}(d_{\delta m}-d_{g})\right)\,,

(8.6a)

with
[
1904
]

\displaystyle Q_{M,\hat{m}}

\displaystyle=0.093-\frac{0.036}{A^{1/3}}-0.02\frac{(A-2Z)^{2}}{A^{2}}-1.4\times 10^{-4}\frac{Z(Z-1)}{A^{4/3}}\,,

(8.6b)

\displaystyle Q_{M,\delta m}

\displaystyle=0.0017\frac{A-2Z}{A}\,,

(8.6c)

\displaystyle Q_{M,m_{e}}

\displaystyle=5.5\times 10^{-4}\frac{Z}{A}\,,

(8.6d)

\displaystyle Q_{M,e}

\displaystyle=\left(-1.4+8.2\frac{Z}{A}+7.7\frac{Z(Z-1)}{A^{4/3}}\right)\times 10^{-4}\,,

(8.6e)

where A and Z are respectively the mass and charge numbers of the atom and where the couplings
d_{i}
are the ones defined in Eq. (
8.3
).

The frequency charge depends on dilatonic charges
Q_{\omega,X}
and on the dilaton/matter coupling coefficients
d_{X}
(see e.g.
[
1989
]
)

\displaystyle[Q^{\mathrm{atom}}_{\omega}]_{d}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\left(Q^{\mathrm{atom}}_{\omega,m_{e}}\left(d_{m_{e}}-d_{g}\right)+Q^{\mathrm{atom}}_{\omega,e}d_{e}+Q^{\mathrm{atom}}_{\omega,\hat{m}}\left(d_{\hat{m}}-d_{g}\right)+Q^{\mathrm{atom}}_{\omega,\delta m}\left(d_{\delta m}-d_{g}\right)\right)\,.

(8.7)

In case of hyperfine transitions, we have
f^{\mathrm{hyp}}_{\mathrm{atom}}\propto\alpha^{k_{\alpha}}(m_{e}/m_{p})(m_{q}/\Lambda_{\mathrm{QCD}})^{k_{q}}

[
1910
]
, where
k_{\alpha},k_{q}
represent respectively the sensitivity coefficients of the hyperfine transition to the fine structure constant and to the ratio of the light quark masses
m_{q}
to the QCD mass scale
\Lambda_{\mathrm{QCD}}
ratio. Subsequently, the corresponding dilatonic frequency charges are

\displaystyle Q^{\mathrm{hyp}}_{\omega,m_{e}}

\displaystyle=1\,,

(8.8a)

\displaystyle Q^{\mathrm{hyp}}_{\omega,e}

\displaystyle=k_{\alpha}\,,

(8.8b)

\displaystyle Q^{\mathrm{hyp}}_{\omega,\hat{m}}

\displaystyle=-0.048+k_{q}\,,

(8.8c)

\displaystyle Q^{\mathrm{hyp}}_{\omega,\delta m}

\displaystyle=0.0017+k_{q}\,,

(8.8d)

where we used the dependency of the proton mass to the light quark masses
\partial\ln m_{p}/\partial\ln\hat{m}=0.048
,
\partial\ln m_{p}/\partial\ln\delta m=-0.0017

[
1904
]
.

The optical transition frequencies depend mainly on the electron mass and on the fine structure constant
f^{\mathrm{opt}}_{\mathrm{atom}}\propto(m_{e}m_{N})\alpha^{2+\epsilon_{\mathrm{atom}}}/(m_{e}+m_{N})

[
1911
,
1912
]
(where
m_{N}
is the total nucleus mass and
\epsilon_{\mathrm{atom}}
accounts for the relativistic correction that determines the frequency dependence on
\alpha
). Therefore, the frequency dilatonic charges write

\displaystyle Q^{\mathrm{opt}}_{\omega,m_{e}}

\displaystyle=1\,,

(8.9a)

\displaystyle Q^{\mathrm{opt}}_{\omega,e}

\displaystyle=2+\epsilon_{\mathrm{atom}}\,,

(8.9b)

\displaystyle Q^{\mathrm{opt}}_{\omega,\hat{m}}

\displaystyle\approx\frac{2.6\times 10^{-5}}{A}\,,

(8.9c)

\displaystyle Q^{\mathrm{opt}}_{\omega,\delta m}

\displaystyle\approx 9.0\times 10^{-7}\frac{A-2Z}{A^{2}}\,,

(8.9d)

where for the third charge
Q^{\mathrm{opt}}_{\omega,\hat{m}}
, we assumed
m_{n}=m_{p}
.

, see Table
8.2
).

8.1.3
UFF violating acceleration of a test mass

The point mass action
[
1904
]

\displaystyle S_{\mathrm{mat}}[\Psi_{i}]=-\sum_{A}\int_{A}d\tau(m_{A}(t,\vec{x})c^{2}+E^{\mathrm{int}}_{A}(t,\vec{x}))\,,

(8.10)

describes the motion of an ensemble of particles with rest mass energy
m_{A}c^{2}
and internal energy
E^{\mathrm{int}}=\hbar\omega_{A}
, with
\omega_{A}
the transition frequency of the body under consideration
1
1

1

This contribution will only be relevant for atoms, see Chapter
13
.
.
\Psi_{i}
are the different SM fields,
d\tau
is the proper time interval defined as
c^{2}d\tau^{2}=-g_{\alpha\beta}dx^{\alpha}dx^{\beta}
, where
g_{\mu\nu}
is the spacetime metric. The body A has its own composition therefore its own coupling with the dilaton field. The equivalent Lagrangian description reads

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{A}

\displaystyle=-\left(m_{A}(t,\vec{x})c+\frac{\hbar\omega_{A}(t,\vec{x})}{c}\right)\sqrt{-g_{\mu\nu}\frac{dx^{\mu}}{dt}\frac{dx^{\nu}}{dt}}\,,

(8.11a)

which, for non relativistic velocities and considering flat spacetime, i.e
\eta_{\mu\nu}=\mathrm{diag}(-1,1,1,1)
, can be approximated by

\mathcal{L}_{A}=-\left(m_{A}(t,\vec{x})c^{2}+\hbar\omega_{A}(t,\vec{x})\right)\left(1-\frac{v_{A}^{2}}{2c^{2}}\right)\,,

(8.11b)

to first order in
(v_{A}/c)^{2}
(where
v_{A}
is the coordinate velocity of the atom).
Using Eq. (
8.5
), a simple Euler-Lagrange derivation of Eq. (
8.11b
) gives

\displaystyle\vec{a}_{A}(t,\vec{x})

\displaystyle=\left[\omega_{\phi}\vec{v}_{A}-\vec{k}_{\phi}c^{2}\right]\frac{\sqrt{16\pi G\rho_{\mathrm{DM}}}}{\omega_{\phi}c}\left([Q^{A}_{M}]_{d}+[Q^{A}_{\omega}]_{d}\frac{\hbar\omega^{0}_{A}}{m^{0}_{A}c^{2}}\right)\sin(\omega_{\phi}t-\vec{k}_{\phi}\cdot\vec{x}+\Phi)\,,

(8.12)

at lowest order in
v_{A}/c
, where the superscript
0
indicates the bare value of the quantity, at zeroth order in the field
\phi
. In addition, we considered the lowest order expansion in factors of
\hbar\omega^{0}_{A}/m^{0}_{A}c^{2}
, as in general
m^{0}_{A}c^{2}\gg\hbar\omega^{0}_{A}
. As we have seen previously, the various
[Q^{A}_{M}]_{d},[Q^{A}_{\omega}]_{d}
charges are atom-dependent, implying that the acceleration Eq. (
8.12
) leads to a violation of the UFF.

8.2
Pseudo-scalar field

8.2.1
Theoretical motivations

The QCD Lagrangian in SM reads

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{QCD}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{4}G^{a}_{\mu\nu}G^{a,\mu\nu}+\bar{\Psi}_{q}\left(i\not{D}-m_{q}e^{i\theta_{q}}\right)\Psi_{q}\,,

(8.13)

where the first term represents the kinetic energy of the non abelian gluon gauge field, through the strength tensors
G^{a}_{\mu\nu}
with
\sqrt{J/m^{3}}
units, the second term represents the interaction between gluons and quarks
\Psi_{q}
(
\not{D}=\gamma^{\mu}D_{\mu}
, where
\gamma^{\mu}
are the dimensionless gamma matrices
2
2

2

The gamma matrices are a set of
4\times 4
matrices which allow one to construct Lorentz scalar with fermionic spinors. These are the 4-dimensional generalization of Pauli matrices.
) and the third term is the quarks mass term. One could add to this Lagrangian a total derivative term (which would not change the fields equations of motion) of the form

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\theta}

\displaystyle=\theta\frac{g^{2}_{3}}{32\pi^{2}}G^{a}_{\mu\nu}\tilde{G}^{a,\mu\nu}\,,

(8.14)

where
\tilde{G}^{a,\mu\nu}=\epsilon^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}G^{a}_{\rho\sigma}/2
is the dual gluon field strength tensor, with superscript
a
running over the 8 gluon fields. Despite leaving the equations of motion invariant, the Lagrangian Eq. (
8.14
) breaks CP (Charge-Parity) symmetry if the
\theta
parameter, being an angle, is different from 0 or
\pi

[
1913
]
. To test CP symmetry of QCD, one can measure the neutron electric dipole moment
d_{n}
whose best upper limit constraint is
|d_{n}|<3\times 10^{-26}
e.cm (90% confidence level)
[
1914
]
, which translates into an upper limit of the
\theta
parameter to be
\theta<10^{-10}

[
1913
]
. This extremely small value is puzzling because we should expect
\theta\sim\mathcal{O}(1)
3
3

3

In reality,
\theta=\theta_{\mathrm{QCD}}+\theta_{q}
where
\theta_{\mathrm{QCD}}
is a contribution from QCD and where
\theta_{q}
is a contribution from electroweak sector (coming from the quark masses), which are two very different sectors, therefore one should not expect those two contributions to cancel each other exactly.
, and makes the so-called strong CP problem.
In the 1970s, Peccei and Quinn
[
1915
]
proposed a solution to solve this problem : promote the previously fixed
\theta
parameter to a dynamical massive field with a potential with a zero minimum, such that
\theta
relaxes naturally to 0
[
1916
,
1917
,
1918
]
.
More precisely, a new
U(1)_{\mathrm{PQ}}
symmetry is introduced which is spontaneously broken at a given energy scale
f_{a}
. The QCD axion is the Goldstone boson of this broken symmetry, which acquires a mass
m_{a}
via its interaction with pions. See
[
1919
]
for a complete review of the Peccei-Quinn mechanism.
One can show that the QCD axion mass is inversely proportional to
f_{a}
, and that it also couples to the photon, nucleon, electron fields and nucleon electric dipole moment with respective couplings
g_{a\gamma},g_{aN}/m_{N}c^{2},g_{ae}/m_{e}c^{2},g_{d}
(with
m_{N},m_{e}
the nucleon and electron mass). All these couplings are proportional to
1/f_{a}
such that the first three have units of 1/energy and the last coupling
g_{d}
has units of
C.m/J
(because it is also proportional to
d_{n}
)
[
1920
,
1921
]
. Therefore, there is only one free parameter of the theory, which is the QCD axion mass. One can define a more generic pseudo-scalar field, the Axion-Like-Particle (ALP)
a
which couples to the same fields and has the same mass as the QCD axion, but where the relation of proportionality between all the parameters disappears, i.e all couplings and mass are free parameters of the theory. Therefore, ALP does not solve the strong CP problem, but is still a DM candidate, as shown in Section
7.3
. The dimensionless ALP field is defined as

\displaystyle\theta=\sqrt{\frac{\hbar c^{5}}{8\pi G}}\frac{a}{f_{a}}\,,

(8.15)

where
\sqrt{\hbar c^{5}/8\pi G}\sim 2\times 10^{18}\>\mathrm{GeV}\equiv E_{P}
is the reduced Planck energy (which is usually defined in terms of the inverse of the
\kappa
parameter, reason for the
8\pi
factor
[
1904
]
) and its full interaction Lagrangian can be written as
[
1920
]

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{int}}

\displaystyle=E_{P}\left(\frac{g^{2}_{3}}{32\pi^{2}}\frac{a}{f_{a}}G^{a}_{\mu\nu}\tilde{G}^{a,\mu\nu}-\frac{g_{a\gamma}}{4\mu_{0}}aF_{\mu\nu}\tilde{F}^{\mu\nu}+\frac{g_{aN}\hbar c}{2m_{N}c^{2}}\partial_{\mu}a\bar{\Psi}_{N}\gamma^{\mu}\gamma_{5}\Psi_{N}+\right.\,

\displaystyle\left.\frac{g_{ae}\hbar c}{2m_{e}c^{2}}\partial_{\mu}a\bar{\Psi}_{e}\gamma^{\mu}\gamma_{5}\Psi_{e}+\frac{c}{2i}g_{d}a\bar{\Psi}_{N}\sigma_{\mu\nu}\gamma_{5}\Psi_{N}F^{\mu\nu}\right)\,,

(8.16)

where
\Psi_{N},\Psi_{e}
are respectively the nucleon and electron fermion fields, where
\gamma_{5}=i\gamma^{0}\gamma^{1}\gamma^{2}\gamma^{3}
the fifth gamma matrix and
\sigma^{\mu\nu}=[\gamma^{\mu},\gamma^{\nu}]/2
.

8.2.2
Axion electrodynamics

Following Eqs. (
7.1b
) and (
8.16
) in flat spacetime (
\sqrt{-g}=1)
and using space positive metric
g_{\mu\nu}=\mathrm{det}(-1,1,1,1)
, we write the Lagrangian describing the interaction between the classical dimensionless axion pseudo scalar field
a
with mass
m_{a}
and EM through the coupling
g_{a\gamma}

[
1913
]

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{4\mu_{0}}F_{\mu\nu}F^{\mu\nu}+j_{\mu}A^{\mu}-\frac{1}{2\kappa}\partial_{\mu}a\partial^{\mu}a-\frac{1}{2\kappa}\frac{m^{2}_{a}c^{2}}{\hbar^{2}}a^{2}-E_{P}\frac{g_{a\gamma}}{4\mu_{0}}aF_{\mu\nu}\tilde{F}^{\mu\nu}\,,

(8.17)

where the EM gauge field
A^{\alpha}
has usual units of V.s/m and where
\tilde{F}^{\mu\nu}
is the dual electromagnetic strength tensor defined as

\displaystyle\tilde{F}^{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\epsilon^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}F_{\rho\sigma}\equiv\epsilon^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}\partial_{\rho}A_{\sigma}\,,

(8.18a)

with
\epsilon^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}
the antisymmetric 4-dimensional Levi-Civita tensor, which has the following properties

\displaystyle\epsilon^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}

\displaystyle=g^{\mu\alpha}g^{\nu\beta}g^{\rho\delta}g^{\sigma\delta}\epsilon_{\alpha\beta\delta\gamma}\,,

(8.18b)

\displaystyle\epsilon_{0ijk}

\displaystyle=\epsilon_{ijk}\,,

(8.18c)

where
\epsilon_{ijk}
is the usual 3-dimensional Levi-Civita tensor. Using these properties, one can show that

\displaystyle F_{\mu\nu}\tilde{F}^{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=4\frac{\vec{E}\cdot\vec{B}}{c}\,,

(8.19)

where we defined
\partial_{\mu}=(\partial_{t}/c,\vec{\nabla})
,
j^{\mu}=(\rho c,\vec{j})
and
A^{\mu}=(\phi/c,\vec{A})
with
\phi,\vec{A}
respectively the scalar and vector potentials and introduce the electric and magnetic fields defined as

\displaystyle\vec{E}

\displaystyle=-\vec{\nabla}\phi-\partial_{t}\vec{A}\,,

(8.20a)

\displaystyle\vec{B}

\displaystyle=\vec{\nabla}\times\vec{A}\,.

(8.20b)

The equation of motion of the axion and photon fields are derived from the usual Euler-Lagrange equations and reads
[
1922
,
1923
]

\displaystyle\left(\square-\frac{m^{2}_{a}c^{2}}{\hbar^{2}}\right)a=\frac{\kappa E_{P}}{\mu_{0}c}g_{a\gamma}\vec{E}\cdot\vec{B}\,,

(8.21a)

for the axion field and

\displaystyle\square{A^{\nu}}-\partial^{\nu}\partial_{\mu}A^{\mu}+E_{P}g_{a\gamma}(\partial_{\mu}a)\epsilon^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}\partial_{\rho}A_{\sigma}

\displaystyle=-\mu_{0}j^{\nu}\,,

(8.21b)

for the photon field.
We can treat separately the temporal and spatial part of Eq. (
8.21b
), i.e

\displaystyle\square{\phi}+\partial_{t}\partial_{\mu}A^{\mu}+cE_{P}g_{a\gamma}\epsilon_{ijk}(\partial_{i}a)\partial_{j}A_{k}

\displaystyle=-\mu_{0}\rho c^{2}\,,

(8.22a)

\displaystyle\square{A^{i}}-\partial^{i}\partial_{\mu}A^{\mu}+E_{P}g_{a\gamma}\epsilon^{\mu i\rho\sigma}(\partial_{\mu}a)\partial_{\rho}A_{\sigma}

\displaystyle=-\mu_{0}j^{i}\,.

(8.22b)

Using the temporal equation Eq. (
8.22a
), the definition of the electric and magnetic fields Eq. (
8.20
) and the zero divergence of the magnetic field, we obtain

\displaystyle\vec{\nabla}\cdot\left(\vec{E}-cE_{P}g_{a\gamma}a\vec{B}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{\rho}{\epsilon_{0}}\,,

(8.23)

which makes the modified Gauss’s law.
From the spatial equation of motion, Eq. (
8.22b
), we obtain

\displaystyle\vec{\nabla}\times\vec{B}=\mu_{0}\left(\vec{j}+\epsilon_{0}\frac{\partial\vec{E}}{\partial t}\right)-\frac{E_{P}}{c}g_{a\gamma}(\dot{a}\vec{B}+\vec{\nabla}a\times\vec{E})\,,

(8.24)

where the dot represents derivative with respect to time and which corresponds to the modified Ampère’s law.

Eqs.(
8.23
) and (
8.24
) together with the two unchanged Maxwell’s equations form what is more commonly known as axion electrodynamics
[
1922
,
1923
]

\displaystyle\vec{\nabla}\cdot\left(\vec{E}-cE_{P}g_{a\gamma}a\vec{B}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{\rho}{\epsilon_{0}}\,,

(8.25a)

\displaystyle\vec{\nabla}\times\vec{E}+\frac{\partial\vec{B}}{\partial t}

\displaystyle=0\,,

(8.25b)

\displaystyle\mu_{0}\left(\vec{j}+\epsilon_{0}\frac{\partial\vec{E}}{\partial t}\right)-\frac{E_{P}}{c}g_{a\gamma}(\dot{a}\vec{B}+\vec{\nabla}a\times\vec{E})

\displaystyle=\vec{\nabla}\times\vec{B}\,,

(8.25c)

\displaystyle\vec{\nabla}.\vec{B}

\displaystyle=0\,.

(8.25d)

The various new terms of Eq. (
8.25
) break the electric-magnetic duality which is present in the usual Maxwell’s equations : in vacuum, these equations are not invariant under the change
(\vec{E},\vec{B})\rightarrow(c\vec{B},-\vec{E}/c)
.

8.2.3
Vacuum birefringence and dichroism

We now focus on the spatial equation of motion of the EM field Eq. (
8.22b
). We assume propagation in vacuum
\vec{j}=0
, both temporal and Coulomb gauges for the EM field
A_{0}=0
and
\vec{\nabla}\cdot\vec{A}=0
4
4

4

Out of the four degrees of freedom encoded in the 4-potential
A^{\mu}
, only 2, representing the photon polarization states, are physical, reason why we can fix two gauges.
and the axion field is spatially homogeneous at the scale of the experiment
5
5

5

As shown in Eq. (
7.28
), the constraint on the axion mass depends on the size of experiment considered.
such that the form Eq. (
7.29
) is valid. Then, Eq. (
8.22b
) becomes
[
1924
]

\displaystyle\ddot{A_{i}}-c^{2}\nabla^{2}A_{i}+cE_{P}g_{a\gamma}\dot{a}\epsilon_{ijk}\partial_{j}A_{k}=0\,.

(8.26)

We can decompose
A_{i}
into the left and right circular polarization modes of the photon with wave number
\vec{k}
as

\displaystyle A_{i}(t,x)=\int\frac{d^{3}k}{(2\pi)^{3}}\left(A^{+}_{k}(t)e^{+}_{i}+A^{-}_{k}(t)e^{-}_{i}\right)e^{i\vec{k}.\vec{x}}\,.

(8.27)

Without loss of generality, if we assume the propagation direction to be along the
\hat{z}
direction,
\vec{k}=(0,0,k)
, the normalized right/left helicities of the photon can be expressed as
e^{+}=e^{R}=(1,-i,0)/\sqrt{2}
and
e^{-}=e^{L}=(1,i,0)/\sqrt{2}
such that

\displaystyle\epsilon_{ijk}k_{j}e^{\pm}_{k}=\pm ike^{\pm}_{i}\,.

(8.28)

Then, we can write two separate equations of motion for both polarization modes
[
1924
]

\displaystyle\ddot{A^{+}_{i}}+k^{2}c^{2}\left(1+\frac{E_{P}}{c^{2}}\frac{\sqrt{16\pi G\rho_{\mathrm{DM}}}g_{a\gamma}}{k}\sin(\omega_{a}t+\Phi)\right)A^{+}_{i}=0\,

(8.29a)

\displaystyle\ddot{A^{-}_{i}}+k^{2}c^{2}\left(1-\frac{E_{P}}{c^{2}}\frac{\sqrt{16\pi G\rho_{\mathrm{DM}}}g_{a\gamma}}{k}\sin(\omega_{a}t+\Phi)\right)A^{-}_{i}=0\,,

(8.29b)

where we used Eq. (
7.17
). Considering the plane wave ansatz
A^{\pm}_{i}(t)=\zeta^{\pm}_{i}\exp(-i(\omega_{\pm}t-\vec{k}\cdot\vec{x}))
, Eq. (
8.29
) leads to

\displaystyle\omega_{\pm}

\displaystyle=kc\sqrt{1\pm\frac{E_{P}}{c^{2}}\frac{\sqrt{16\pi G\rho_{\mathrm{DM}}}g_{a\gamma}}{k}\sin(\omega_{a}t+\Phi)}\,,

(8.30)

or in other words, the dispersion relation of the right/left circular polarizations is modified such that they travel with different phase velocities
c_{\pm}=\omega_{\pm}/k
, respectively

\displaystyle c_{\pm}=c\sqrt{1\pm\frac{E_{P}}{c^{2}}\frac{\sqrt{16\pi G\rho_{\mathrm{DM}}}g_{a\gamma}}{k}\sin(\omega_{a}t+\Phi)}\,.

(8.31)

Note that Eq. (
8.30
) leads also to a modification of the group velocity of the left and right polarization of light

\displaystyle v_{g}

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial\omega_{\pm}}{\partial k}\approx c\left(1+\frac{16\pi G\rho_{\mathrm{DM}}E^{2}_{P}g^{2}_{a\gamma}}{8k^{2}c^{4}}\sin^{2}\left(\omega_{a}t+\Phi\right)\right)+\mathcal{O}\left(\left(\frac{E_{P}}{c^{2}}\frac{\sqrt{16\pi G\rho_{\mathrm{DM}}}g_{a\gamma}}{k}\right)^{3}\right)\,.

(8.32)

This modification of the group velocity does not break special relativity since we now assume that light propagates inside a medium full of axions, which is not pure vacuum.

In other words, vacuum becomes birefringent in presence of an axion background. Experiments such as
DANCE

[
1925
]
,
LIDA

[
1926
]
or
ADBC

[
1927
]
attempt to detect axion-photon coupling through this effect.

We now study dichroism effects from axion-photon coupling. To do so, we focus specifically on the axion-photon interaction Lagrangian, written in terms of electric and magnetic fields i.e

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{a\gamma}

\displaystyle=-E_{P}\frac{g_{a\gamma}}{\mu_{0}c}a\vec{E}\cdot\vec{B}\,.

(8.33)

In a medium of permittivity
\epsilon
and permeability
\mu
and where external electric and magnetic fields
\vec{E}_{0}
and
\vec{B}_{0}
are applied, the modified Maxwell’s equations Eqs. (
8.23
) and (
8.24
) show that the axion field is a source of electric charge
\rho_{a}
and current density
\vec{j}_{a}

[
1913
]

\displaystyle\rho_{a}

\displaystyle=\epsilon cE_{P}g_{a\gamma}\vec{\nabla}a\cdot\vec{B}_{0}\,

(8.34a)

\displaystyle\vec{j}_{a}

\displaystyle=\frac{\mu E_{P}}{c}g_{a\gamma}\left(\dot{a}\vec{B}_{0}+\vec{\nabla}a\times\vec{E}_{0}\right)\,,

(8.34b)

implying a conversion between axions and photons.

As derived in
[
1913
]
, if we assume light propagating in the
z
direction whose polarization lies in the
x-y
plane and a static and homogeneous magnetic field polarized along the
x
axis
\vec{B}_{0}\equiv B_{0}\hat{e}_{x}
, with no electric field, the photon-axion conversion will happen in the same direction as the B polarization direction. This means that the polarization component of light along the
x
-direction is depleted, implying a rotation of the plane of polarization. This is what is called dichroism. The conversion probability is given by
[
1913
]

\displaystyle P(\gamma\rightarrow a,L)

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\frac{\mu}{\epsilon}}\sqrt{\frac{\epsilon_{0}}{\mu_{0}}}\frac{\hbar}{\mu_{0}}\frac{g^{2}_{a\gamma}B^{2}_{0}}{\beta_{a}q^{2}}\sin^{2}\left(\frac{qL}{2}\right)\,,

(8.35a)

where
L
is the travelling distance, q is the difference in wavevector between axions and photons, i.e
q=|\vec{k}_{a}-\vec{k}_{\gamma}|
and
\beta_{a}=|\vec{k}_{a}|/\omega_{a}\equiv v_{a}/c^{2}
with
v_{a}
the speed of incident axions. Note that, by energy conservation,
\omega_{a}=\omega_{\gamma}
, since the static magnetic field does not transfer energy.

In addition, the relative phase between the polarizations parallel and perpendicular to the magnetic field changes, and light acquires ellipticity
[
1913
]
. If we assume plane wave solutions for axions and photons, one can show that the
\gamma\rightarrow a
conversion leads to a modification of the dispersion relation of photons of
x
polarization, which depends on the amplitude of the magnetic field in this direction. This is equivalent to a relative phase added to the
x
-polarization compared to the
y
-one that reads
[
1913
]

\displaystyle\phi(L)

\displaystyle=\frac{\hbar c}{\mu_{0}}\frac{g^{2}_{a\gamma}B^{2}_{0}}{4q^{2}}\left(qL-\sin(qL)\right)\,.

(8.35b)

8.2.4
UFF violation through the coupling with gluons

We now consider the coupling between the ALP and gluons which reads, from Eq. (
8.16
),

\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{int}}=E_{P}\frac{g^{2}_{3}}{32\pi^{2}}\frac{a}{f_{a}}G^{a}_{\mu\nu}\tilde{G}^{a,\mu\nu}\equiv\frac{g^{2}_{3}}{32\pi^{2}}\theta G^{a}_{\mu\nu}\tilde{G}^{a,\mu\nu}\,.

(8.36)

In
[
1912
]
, it is shown that the interaction Lagrangian from Eq. (
8.36
) induces a dependency of the mass of pions to the axion field, which implies a dependency of the mass of nucleons and atomic binding energy on the axion field. More precisely,

\frac{\partial\ln m_{N}}{\partial\left(\theta^{2}\right)}=\frac{\partial\ln m_{N}}{\partial\ln\left(m_{\pi}^{2}\right)}\frac{\partial\ln\left(m_{\pi}^{2}\right)}{\partial\left(\theta^{2}\right)}\,,

(8.37)

where
m_{N}
is the rest mass of the nucleon N. As a consequence, the mass of any atom will also depend on the axion field and its coupling strength with gluons. In this sense, we can define the dimensionless axionic mass and frequency charges of an atom A as

\displaystyle[Q^{\mathrm{atom}}_{M}]_{a}

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial\ln m_{\mathrm{atom}}}{\partial\left(\theta^{2}\right)}\,

(8.38a)

\displaystyle[Q^{\mathrm{atom}}_{\omega}]_{a}

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial\ln\omega_{\mathrm{atom}}}{\partial\left(\theta^{2}\right)}\,,

(8.38b)

in a similar way as in Eq. (
8.4
). Therefore, a similar oscillation on both rest mass and transition frequency of an atom A arise

\displaystyle m_{A}(t,\vec{x})

\displaystyle=m^{0}_{A}\left(1+\frac{8\pi G\rho_{\mathrm{DM}}E^{2}_{P}[Q^{A}_{M}]_{a}}{f^{2}_{a}\omega^{2}_{a}c^{2}}\cos(2(\omega_{a}t-\vec{k}_{a}\cdot\vec{x}+\Phi))\right)\,,

(8.39a)

\displaystyle\omega_{A}(t,\vec{x})

\displaystyle=\omega^{0}_{A}\left(1+\frac{8\pi G\rho_{\mathrm{DM}}E^{2}_{P}[Q^{A}_{\omega}]_{a}}{f^{2}_{a}\omega^{2}_{a}c^{2}}\cos(2(\omega_{a}t-\vec{k}_{a}\cdot\vec{x}+\Phi))\right)\,.

(8.39b)

We made a redefinition of the unperturbed mass as
m^{0}_{A}\rightarrow m^{0}_{A}(1+8\pi G\rho_{\mathrm{DM}}E^{2}_{P}[Q^{A}_{\omega}]_{a}/f^{2}_{a}\omega^{2}_{a}c^{2})
, and similarly for the frequency. Then, the axion-gluon coupling would induce a UFF violating acceleration on the atom A of the form

\displaystyle\vec{a}_{A}(t,\vec{x})=\left[\omega_{a}\vec{v}_{A}-\vec{k}_{a}c^{2}\right]\frac{16\pi G\rho_{\mathrm{DM}}E^{2}_{P}}{f^{2}_{a}\omega^{2}_{a}c^{2}}\left([Q^{A}_{M}]_{a}+[Q^{A}_{\omega}]_{a}\frac{\hbar\omega^{0}_{A}}{m^{0}_{A}c^{2}}\right)\sin\left(2\omega_{a}t-2\vec{k}_{a}\cdot\vec{x}+2\Phi\right)\,,

(8.40)

where we did not include contributions from the axion spin-induced fifth force (see e.g.
[
1928
]
), because we are only interested in the oscillating solution.
Now, we wish to get an analytical expression for both axionic charges.

The rest mass of an atom, with charge number Z and neutron number N, can be parameterized as

\displaystyle m_{\mathrm{atom}}c^{2}

\displaystyle=m_{\mathrm{const.}}c^{2}+E_{\mathrm{bind}}\equiv Z(m_{p}+m_{e})c^{2}+Nm_{n}c^{2}+E_{\mathrm{bind}}\,,

(8.41)

where
m_{p},m_{e},m_{n}
are respectively the rest masses of the proton, the electron and the neutron, and where
m_{\mathrm{const.}},E_{\mathrm{bind}}
represent respectively the rest mass of the particle constituents of the atom (proton, neutron, electron) and the nuclear binding energy.

Nucleons rest mass

In
[
1912
]
, it is shown the pion mass is
\theta
dependent and influence the nucleon mass
m_{\mathrm{N}}
through

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\ln m_{N}}{\partial\ln m_{\pi}^{2}}

\displaystyle\approx 0.06\,,

(8.42a)

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\ln m_{\pi}^{2}}{\partial\left(\theta^{2}\right)}

\displaystyle=-\frac{m_{u}m_{d}}{2(m_{u}+m_{d})^{2}}=-0.109\,

(8.42b)

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\frac{\partial\ln m_{N}}{\partial\left(\theta^{2}\right)}

\displaystyle\approx-0.065\,,

(8.42c)

such that, for an atom made of (N+Z) nucleons, the contribution of the nucleons rest mass to the atom rest mass to the axionic charge is given by

[Q^{\mathrm{atom}}_{M}]_{a}\Big|_{\mathrm{const.}}=\frac{\partial\ln m_{N}}{\partial\left(\theta^{2}\right)}\approx-0.065\,,

(8.43)

meaning that it is independent of the number of nucleons inside the atom, or in other words of the atomic species.

Binding energy of the nuclei

Let us now focus on the contribution of the binding energy of the nuclei to the axionic mass charge and show that it is composition dependent.

As computed in
[
1904
]
, the binding energy of the nuclei depends to first order on the mass of the pions and therefore, following Eq. (
8.42
), on the ALP. We will now use the results from Section IV of
[
1904
]
to infer the analytical expression of the dependency of the binding energy to the ALP and to the mass number
A
and the charge number
Z
.

Four different interactions contribute to the binding energy
[
1904
]
: the central force
E_{\mathrm{central}}
coming from the isospin symmetric central nuclear force, the asymmetry energy
E_{\mathrm{asym}}
, i.e the residual energy from the asymmetry between neutrons and protons inside the nucleus, the Coulomb force
E_{\mathrm{Coulomb}}
depending on how tightly the nucleons are packed together and the pairing energy
E_{\mathrm{pairing}}
leading to

\displaystyle E_{\mathrm{bind}}

\displaystyle=E_{\mathrm{central}}+E_{\mathrm{asym}}\frac{(A-2Z)^{2}}{A}+E_{\mathrm{Coulomb}}\frac{Z(Z-1)}{A^{1/3}}-\delta\frac{E_{\mathrm{pairing}}}{A^{1/2}}\,,

(8.44)

where
\delta=\pm 1
respectively for even-even and odd-odd number of protons-neutrons in the nuclei, and
\delta=0
otherwise.

Central force

This interaction comes from the isospin symmetric central nuclear force, which is the dominant contribution in the binding of heavy nuclei
[
1904
]
.
The dominant interactions are an attractive scalar
\eta_{S}
and a repulsive vector
\eta_{V}
, and
[
1904
]
shows that the former is more sensitive to the pion mass, implying in our case

\displaystyle\frac{\partial E_{\mathrm{Central}}}{\partial(\theta^{2})}\approx\frac{\partial E_{\mathrm{Central}}}{\partial\eta_{S}}\frac{\partial\eta_{S}}{\partial\ln m^{2}_{\pi}}\frac{\partial\ln m^{2}_{\pi}}{\partial(\theta^{2})}\,,

(8.45a)

with
[
1929
,
1904
]

\displaystyle E_{\mathrm{Central}}

\displaystyle\approx-(120A-97A^{2/3})\eta_{S}\,,

(8.45b)

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\eta_{S}}{\partial\ln m^{2}_{\pi}}

\displaystyle=-0.35\mathrm{\ MeV}\,.

(8.45c)

Asymmetry energy

The residual energy from the asymmetry between neutrons and protons inside the nucleus contains two components : 1) from the Pauli exclusion principle, requiring that when there are more neutrons than protons, the extra neutrons are in the higher energy states than the protons; and 2) from the nuclear force, which is more attractive for a neutron and a proton than with a pair of neutrons or a pair of protons.
The asymmetry energy depends mainly on the scalar coupling strength between the nucleons
G_{S}

[
1904
]
implying

\displaystyle\frac{\partial E_{\mathrm{Asym}}}{\partial(\theta^{2})}=\frac{\partial E_{\mathrm{Asym}}}{\partial G_{S}}\frac{\partial G_{S}}{\partial\ln m^{2}_{\pi}}\frac{\partial\ln m^{2}_{\pi}}{\partial(\theta^{2})}\,,

(8.46a)

with
[
1904
,
1929
]

\displaystyle\frac{\partial E_{\mathrm{Asym}}}{\partial G_{S}}\frac{\partial G_{S}}{\partial\ln m^{2}_{\pi}}

\displaystyle=-19\mathrm{\ MeV}\,.

(8.46b)

Coulomb force

The Coulomb energy has a dependency on the strong interaction coupling terms since it depends on how tightly the nucleons are packed together. It can be shown that this contribution depends on the same scalar coupling as the asymmetry energy, hence

\displaystyle\frac{\partial E_{\mathrm{Coulomb}}}{\partial(\theta^{2})}=\frac{\partial E_{\mathrm{Coulomb}}}{\partial G_{S}}\frac{\partial G_{S}}{\partial\ln m^{2}_{\pi}}\frac{\partial\ln m^{2}_{\pi}}{\partial(\theta^{2})}\,,

(8.47a)

with
[
1904
,
1929
]

\displaystyle\frac{\partial E_{\mathrm{Coulomb}}}{\partial G_{S}}\frac{\partial G_{S}}{\partial\ln m^{2}_{\pi}}

\displaystyle=-0.13\mathrm{\ MeV}\,.

(8.47b)

Pairing energy

The pairing interaction contributes to binding energy and its numerical value is
[
1929
]

E_{\mathrm{Pairing}}=12\mathrm{\ MeV}\,.

(8.48)

[
1904
]
shows that this contribution is subdominant for all atoms, due to its dependency to mass number A, compared to the other interactions, hence we will not consider the pairing energy in the calculation of the axionic charges of atoms.

Adding all contributions together, we get the analytic expression for the axionic mass charge

\displaystyle[Q^{\mathrm{atom}}_{M}]_{a}=\frac{1}{m_{\mathrm{atom}}}\frac{\partial m_{\mathrm{atom}}}{\partial(\theta^{2})}\,

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{m_{\mathrm{atom}}c^{2}}\left(\frac{\partial(m_{\mathrm{rest\ mass}}c^{2})}{\partial(\theta^{2})}+\frac{\partial E_{\mathrm{bind}}}{\partial(\theta^{2})}\right)\,

\displaystyle\approx-0.065\frac{m_{\mathrm{rest\ mass}}}{m_{\mathrm{atom}}}+\frac{1\mathrm{\>MeV}}{m_{\mathrm{atom}}c^{2}}\left(-4.578A+3.701A^{2/3}+2.071\frac{(A-2Z)^{2}}{A}+0.014\frac{Z(Z-1)}{A^{1/3}}\right)\,

\displaystyle\approx-0.065+F_{A}\Big(-4.92+\frac{3.98}{A^{1/3}}+2.22\frac{(A-2Z)^{2}}{A^{2}}+1.50\frac{Z(Z-1)}{A^{4/3}}\times 10^{-2}\Big)\times 10^{-3}\,.

(8.49)

At the last line, we considered
m_{\mathrm{rest\ mass}}/m_{\mathrm{atom}}\approx 1+\langle E_{\mathrm{bind}}\rangle/m_{\mathrm{amu}}c^{2}
with
\langle E_{\mathrm{bind}}\rangle\sim 8
MeV, the average binding energy per nucleon and
m_{\mathrm{amu}}
the atomic mass unit
[
1904
]
. Also, we factorized
F_{A}=Am_{\mathrm{amu}}/m_{\mathrm{atom}}
which is of order unity at first order for all species of atoms (the relative error is
\mathcal{O}(10^{-3})
)
[
1904
]
.

Now, we focus on the axionic frequency charge.
We will first consider hyperfine atomic transitions (
\omega^{\mathrm{hyp}}_{\mathrm{atom}}
) which are impacted by the axion-gluon coupling from Eq. (
8.36
) as
[
1912
]

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\ln\omega^{\mathrm{hyp}}_{\mathrm{atom}}}{\partial(\theta)^{2}}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{\partial\ln g}{\partial\ln m^{2}_{\pi}}-\frac{\partial\ln m_{p}}{\partial\ln m^{2}_{\pi}}\right)\frac{\partial\ln m^{2}_{\pi}}{\partial(\theta)^{2}}\,,

(8.50)

where
g
is the nucleon g-factor whose dependence on pion mass is given by
[
1912
]

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\ln g}{\partial\ln m^{2}_{\pi}}

\displaystyle=K_{n}\frac{\partial\ln g_{n}}{\partial\ln m^{2}_{\pi}}+K_{p}\frac{\partial\ln g_{p}}{\partial\ln m^{2}_{\pi}}-0.17K_{b}\,,

(8.51a)

with

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\ln g_{n}}{\partial\ln m^{2}_{\pi}}

\displaystyle\approx-0.25\,,

(8.51b)

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\ln g_{p}}{\partial\ln m^{2}_{\pi}}

\displaystyle\approx-0.17\,,

(8.51c)

where
g_{n}
(
g_{p}
) respectively the neutron (proton) gyromagnetic factors and
K_{n}
,
K_{p}
and
K_{b}
, coefficients computed from chiral perturbation theory (nuclear shell model). These coefficients are not measurable, but they are related to observable parameters, namely, the sensitivity coefficient of the nuclear magnetic moment to light quarks masses
\kappa_{q}
, to strange quarks masses
\kappa_{s}
and to light quarks masses over QCD energy scale
\kappa
through
[
1910
]

\displaystyle\kappa_{q}

\displaystyle=-0.118K_{n}-0.087K_{p}\,,

(8.52a)

\displaystyle\kappa_{s}

\displaystyle=0.0013K_{n}-0.013K_{p}\,,

(8.52b)

\displaystyle\kappa

\displaystyle=-0.12K_{n}-0.10K_{p}-0.11K_{b}\,.

(8.52c)

Using Eqs. (
8.42
), (
8.50
), (
8.51
) and (
8.52
), we define the dimensionless axionic frequency charge of the atom as

\displaystyle[Q^{\mathrm{atom}}_{\omega}]_{a}

\displaystyle\approx\left(-16.8\kappa-5.69\kappa_{q}+25.1\kappa_{s}+0.65\right)\times 10^{-2}\,.

(8.53)

In particular, Eq. (
8.53
) is relevant for
87
Rb hyperfine transition, whose associated
\kappa
parameters are
\kappa=-0.016,\kappa_{q}=-0.046,\kappa_{s}=-0.010

[
1910
]
.

Table 8.3
:
Axionic charges for some species of atoms. The transition is hyperfine for
87
Rb and optical for the rest (as in Table
8.1
, all of the optical transition are the
{}^{1}S_{0}\rightarrow^{3}P_{1}
ICL transition, see Table
8.2
). The charges are derived from Eqs. (
8.49
), (
8.53
), (
8.54
) and (
8.55
). As we shall see in Chapters
13
and
15
, we will not consider the atomic transition of
195
Pt,
48
Ti, SiO
2
and Au-Pt, reason why their frequency charge is not provided.

Let us now consider optical transitions whose frequency does depend neither on the nucleon g-factor nor on the proton mass at lowest order. This implies that the axionic frequency charge at lowest order is 0. Higher order contributions would lead to non-zero axionic frequency charge, in particular if one considers its dependence on the fine structure constant
\alpha
which arises at loop level
[
1930
]
, or to the nuclear charge radius
[
1931
]
and are therefore highly suppressed. We now compute the axionic optical frequency charge of an atom A, considering both of these effects for some atomic optical transition. Using the dependence of optical transition on the fine structure constant
\alpha
, the gluon-photon coupling at loop level leads to a charge

\displaystyle[Q^{A}_{\omega}]_{a}

\displaystyle=c_{F_{2}}\frac{(2+\epsilon_{A})\alpha}{4\pi^{2}}\,,

(8.54a)

where
c_{F_{2}}
is the parameter encoding the explicit symmetry-breaking, generating the one-loop coupling. While for QCD axion, this parameter is
\mathcal{O}(1)
, it is much smaller in the case of ALP (due to the introduction of an additional dark sector, e.g a dark photon, see
[
1930
]
). It is nevertheless possible to have
c_{F_{2}}\sim 10^{-2}

[
1930
]
. Therefore, the corresponding axionic charge is, e.g. for
171
Yb,

\displaystyle[Q^{Yb}_{\omega}]_{a}

\displaystyle\sim 1.2\times 10^{-8}\,.

(8.54b)

The second contribution, arising from the coupling of the ALP to the nuclear charge radius has the form
[
1931
,
1932
,
1933
]

\displaystyle[Q^{A}_{\omega}]_{a}

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial\ln\omega_{A}}{\partial\ln\langle r^{2}_{N}\rangle}\frac{\partial\ln\langle r^{2}_{N}\rangle}{\partial\ln m^{2}_{\pi}}\frac{\partial\ln m^{2}_{\pi}}{\partial(\theta)^{2}}=\frac{\beta F}{\omega_{A}}\frac{\partial\ln m^{2}_{\pi}}{\partial(\theta)^{2}}\left(\langle r^{2}_{N}\rangle+\frac{C_{2}}{C_{1}}\langle r^{4}_{N}\rangle\right)\,,

(8.55a)

where
\langle r^{2}_{N}\rangle,\langle r^{4}_{N}\rangle
are respectively the mean squared and quartic charge nuclear radius,
F
is the difference in field shift factor between excited and ground state,
\beta=\partial\ln\langle r^{2}_{N}\rangle/\partial\ln m^{2}_{\pi}\equiv-0.2

[
1931
]
and
C_{1},C_{2}
are Seltzer coefficients, to account for higher order radial moments in the computation of the field shift (which are relevant for heavy atoms)
[
1933
]
. For
171
Yb optical transition,
F=-2\pi\times 10.955
GHz/fm
2

[
1934
]
,
r_{N}=5.2906
fm
[
1935
]
,
C_{1}=6.23\times 10^{-4}
and
C_{2}=-0.579

[
1932
]
, which leads to

\displaystyle[Q^{Yb}_{\omega}]_{a}

\displaystyle\sim-1.21\times 10^{-5}\,.

(8.55b)

For other atomic transition, we will use the fact that the field shift
F\langle r^{2}_{N}\rangle
approximately scales as
Z^{2}/A^{1/3}

[
1933
]
.
Note that, as expected, those two contributions are at least two orders of magnitude smaller than the axionic charges of hyperfine transitions (see Table
8.3
).

8.3
Vector field

8.3.1
Theoretical motivations

In addition to the current SM gauge group
SU(3)\times SU(2)\times U(1)
, many theories predict an additional gauge group which would resolve various particle physics and/or cosmology problems, e.g. the
\nu
MSM model solves DM, neutrino masses and baryogenesis by incorporating three right-handed
SU(2)_{R}\times U(1)
neutrinos
[
1936
]
. The minimal gauged extension would be an additional
U(1)
symmetry, carried by a new vector field, coupled to a local gauge symmetry current, in particular a linear combination of the baryon number
B
, the lepton number
L
and the hypercharge
Y

[
1937
,
1938
]
. Supersymmetry predicts a coupling to the
B-L
current
[
1937
,
1938
]
. This new vector field, sometimes referred as
U
-boson, heavy photon or dark photon (DP), would induce a new force, proportional to the current to which it couples.

8.3.2
Oscillating electric field through the coupling with photons

We now are interested with the interaction of the DP vector field
\phi^{\mu}
of mass
m_{U}
with EM. Using Eq. (
7.1c
), the Lagrangian describing such interaction is given by
[
2148
]

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{4\mu_{0}}F^{\mu\nu}F_{\mu\nu}+j^{\mu}A_{\mu}-\frac{1}{4\mu_{0}}\phi^{\mu\nu}\phi_{\mu\nu}-\frac{m^{2}_{U}c^{2}}{2\mu_{0}\hbar^{2}}\phi^{\mu}\phi_{\mu}-\frac{\chi}{2\mu_{0}}F_{\mu\nu}\phi^{\mu\nu}\,,

(8.56)

where
\chi
is the dimensionless kinetic mixing coupling parameter which characterizes the coupling between the DP and the EM field.

In order to ease the calculations, we make a non-unitary transformation of the fields, i.e
[
1940
]

\bar{A}^{\mu}=A^{\mu}+\chi\phi^{\mu}

(8.57)

while
\phi^{\mu}
remains unchanged at first order in
\chi
. This allows us to redefine the fields in terms of mass eigenstates called massless and massive photons, which couples to the standard EM current. Using this change of variable, Eq. (
8.56
) becomes, at first order in
\chi
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{4\mu_{0}}\bar{F}^{\mu\nu}\bar{F}_{\mu\nu}+j^{\mu}\left(\bar{A}_{\mu}-\chi\phi_{\mu}\right)-\frac{1}{4\mu_{0}}\phi^{\mu\nu}\phi_{\mu\nu}-\frac{m^{2}_{U}c^{2}}{2\mu_{0}\hbar^{2}}\phi^{\mu}\phi_{\mu}\,,

(8.58)

with
F_{\mu\nu}=\bar{F}_{\mu\nu}-\chi\phi_{\mu\nu}
. The field equations read

\displaystyle\partial_{\alpha}\bar{F}^{\beta\alpha}

\displaystyle=\mu_{0}j^{\beta}\,,

(8.59a)

\displaystyle\partial_{\alpha}\phi^{\beta\alpha}

\displaystyle=-\chi\mu_{0}j^{\beta}-\frac{m^{2}_{U}c^{2}}{\hbar^{2}}\phi^{\beta}\,.

(8.59b)

The antisymmetry of both strength tensors leads to the conservation of the electromagnetic 4-current
\partial_{\mu}j^{\mu}=0
, and the continuity equation for the DP field
\partial_{\mu}\phi^{\mu}=0
, as shown in Section
7.3.2
.

Using the Lorenz gauge for EM,
\partial^{\mu}\bar{A}_{\mu}=0
, Eqs. (
8.59
) become

\displaystyle\Box\bar{A}^{\beta}

\displaystyle=-\mu_{0}j^{\beta}\,,

(8.60a)

\displaystyle\Box\phi^{\beta}

\displaystyle=\frac{m^{2}_{U}c^{2}}{\hbar^{2}}\phi^{\beta}+\chi\mu_{0}j^{\beta}\,,

(8.60b)

where
\Box=\eta^{\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}\partial_{\nu}\equiv-\frac{1}{c^{2}}\partial^{2}_{t}+\nabla^{2}
. These equations
admit two classes of solutions in vacuum (where the current vanishes): a massless vector field (standard EM) and a massive one, the latter being characterized by solutions :

\displaystyle\phi^{\beta}

\displaystyle=Y^{\beta}e^{ik_{\mu}x^{\mu}+\Phi}\,,

(8.61a)

\displaystyle\bar{A}^{\alpha}

\displaystyle=0\,,

(8.61b)

where
k_{\mu}k^{\mu}=-(\omega_{U}/c)^{2}+\left|\vec{k}_{U}\right|^{2}=-m^{2}_{U}c^{2}/\hbar^{2}
, as derived in Eq. (
7.7b
), and where Eq. (
8.61a
) is equivalent to Eq. (
7.8
).
As can be noticed from Eq. (
8.57
), this solution induces an ordinary electromagnetic field in a vacuum (see also
[
2148
]
)

A^{\beta}=-\chi Y^{\beta}e^{ik_{\mu}x^{\mu}+\Phi}\,.

(8.62)

Due to the coupling between the DP and the EM field (coupling characterized by the mixing parameter
\chi
), the DP field will induce a small electromagnetic field, whose strength is proportional to
\chi
and to the DP field amplitude, see Eq. (
8.62
). Then the induced electromagnetic field consists in an oscillating electric and magnetic fields of the form
[
2148
]

\displaystyle E^{j}_{\mathrm{DM}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{\partial A^{0}}{\partial x_{j}}-\frac{\partial A^{j}}{\partial t}=\chi\left(k^{j}_{U}Y^{0}-\omega_{U}Y^{j}\right)\sin\left(\omega_{U}t-\vec{k}_{U}\cdot\vec{x}+\Phi\right)\,

(8.63a)

\displaystyle B^{j}_{\mathrm{DM}}

\displaystyle=\epsilon_{ijk}\partial^{j}A^{k}=-\chi\epsilon_{ijk}k^{j}_{U}Y^{k}\sin\left(\omega_{U}t-\vec{k}_{U}\cdot\vec{x}+\Phi\right)\,,

(8.63b)

The magnetic component is suppressed by a factor
v_{\mathrm{DM}}/c\sim 10^{-3}
